Text Data Mining

Hyunah Ahn

Intro

Text Data Mining

  • Course Objectives: By the end of the semester, Ss will be able to…

    • Preprocess text data for analysis using R (or Python)

    • Visualize relationships between words and documents

    • Apply computational linguistics concepts (Zipf’s law, tf-idf, LDA) to conducting sentiment analysis and topic modeling

  • Data: Texts in Project Gutenberg

  • Software: R (or Python)

The course assumes no prior knowledge on programming or statistics.

Weekly Topics

  • Week 1: Course overview

  • Week 2: How to use R (and Rstudio)

  • Week 3: Project Gutenberg

  • Week 4: Understanding “tidy text” and preprocessing text.

  • Week 5: Tokenizing text

  • Week 6: Word frequency and Zipf’s law

  • Week 7: Midterm

  • Week 8: Project Workshop

  • Week 9: Data visualization

  • Week 10: Sentiment Analysis

  • Week 11: Term Frequency (and Inverse Document Frequency)

  • Week 12: Latent Dirichlet Allocation

  • Week 13: Topic Modeling

  • Week 14: Presentations

  • Week 15: Final exam

Review

Project Gutenberg

  • Free Digital Library: A collection of over 70,000 free eBooks, including classic literature and historical texts (www.gutenberg.org)

  • Accessible Text Data: Invaluable for linguistics and text analysis (plain-text versions of books, easy to process computationally)

  • Packages available in both R and python: gutenbergr (R) and Gutenberg (python)

Pride & Prejudice

library(janeaustenr)
library(tidytext)
library(tidyverse)
pride <- janeaustenr::austen_books()%>%
  filter(book=="Pride & Prejudice")
print(pride, n=15)

The raw form of data is “messy” and cannot be analyzed as is.

Tidying & Preprocessing Text

pride1<-pride %>%
  # Detect lines that mark the beginning of a chapter
  mutate(chapter_start = str_detect(text, regex("^(Chapter|CHAPTER)\\s+[IVXLCDM0-9]+", ignore_case = TRUE)))%>%
  # Assign a chapter number based on where chapters start
  mutate(chapter = cumsum(chapter_start)) %>%
  # Remove the temporary chapter_start column
  select(book, chapter, text) %>%
  # Filter out any pre-chapter text or rows without valid chapter content
  filter(chapter > 0)%>%
  mutate(text = str_replace_all(text, "[:punct:]|\\d", " ")) %>%
  filter(!str_detect(text, regex("^\\s*$")), # Remove empty lines
         !str_detect(text, regex("^(Chapter|CHAPTER)\\s", ignore_case = TRUE)) )%>% # Remove chapter titles
  mutate(text = str_squish(text)) %>%
  select(-chapter)

print(pride1, n=10)
# A tibble: 10,652 × 2
   book              text                                                       
   <fct>             <chr>                                                      
 1 Pride & Prejudice It is a truth universally acknowledged that a single man i…

Tokenizing Text

pride_words<-pride1%>%
  unnest_tokens(word, text)

print(pride_words, n=15)
# A tibble: 122,750 × 2
   book              word

What is tidy data?

  1. A column is a variable.
  2. A row is an observation.
  3. A cell is a value.

“Happy families are all alike; every unhappy family is unhappy in its own way.” — Leo Tolstoy

“Tidy datasets are all alike, but every messy dataset is messy in its own way.” — Hadley Wickham

In the previous five weeks, the course has covered the topics just briefed.

Counting words in “Pride & Prejudice”

pride_words<-pride1%>%
  unnest_tokens(word, text)

print(pride_words, n=15)

  • Number of Types (w/o repeats)
type<-length(unique(pride_words$word))

6257 words

  • Number of Tokens (w/ repeats)
token<-nrow(pride_words)

122750 words

  • Type-Token Ratio
ttr<-round((type/token)*100, digits = 2)

5.1% \(\approx\) 1:20

  • Q. How many words were used more than 4000 times? 1000 times?
  • Q. How about fewer than 10 times? Or just once?

Proportion of frequent and rare words

Words used > 1000 times

thousand<-pride_wordCount%>%filter(n>1000); ttype<-nrow(thousand)
fst<-sum(thousand$n)
type22<-round((ttype/nrow(pride_wordCount))*100, digits = 2)
token22<-round((fst/nrow(pride_words))*100, digits = 2)
  • 22 unique words (types)
  • Total number of types = 6257
  • 22/6257 = 0.35%
  • Frequency sum of 22 words = 42596
  • Total number of tokens = 122750
  • 42596/122750 = 34.7%

Words used Fewer than 10 times

fewer10<-pride_wordCount%>%filter(n<10); btype<-nrow(fewer10)
fsb<-sum(fewer10$n)
typeb<-round((btype/nrow(pride_wordCount))*100, digits = 2)
tokenb<-round((fsb/nrow(pride_words))*100, digits = 2)

  • 5048 unique words (types)

  • Total number of types = 6257

  • 5048/6257 = 80.68%

  • Frequency sum of infreq. words = 12838

  • Total number of tokens = 122750

  • 12838/122750 = 10.46%

Distribution of words in a large text

  • Frequent words
    • 0.35% (22 words) of all unique words (types = 6257) are used more than 1000 times and make up around 35% of all tokens (= 122,750).
  • Infrequent words
    • Around 80% (5048 words) of all unique words (types) are used fewer than 10 times and make up only around 10% of the entire text (tokens).
  • Zipfian distribution
    • In a large text, a small number of unique words occur very frequently, while the majority occur rarely, creating a predictable imbalance between word frequency and diversity.

Discussion Questions

Frequent Words

# Words used more than 1000 times
paste(thousand$word, collapse = ", ")
[1] "the, to, of, and, her, i, a, in, was, she, that, it, not, you, he, his, be, as, had, for, with, but"

Infrequent Words

# A few examples of words used only once
paste(fewer10$word[5029:5048], collapse = ", ")
[1] "wisdom, wiser, wisher, withdrawing, withstood, witnessing, wits, witticisms, witty, wives, woe, womanly, worthlessness, wretchedly, writes, yards, yawned, yawning, york, youths"
  • Which words matter more?
  • What would happen if we removed all frequent words?
  • What would happen if we removed all infrequent words?

Ranking word frequency

pride_wordCount1<-pride_wordCount%>%
  mutate(total = sum(n))%>%
  mutate(tf = n/total)%>%
  mutate(rank = rank(-n, ties.method = "min"))
 pride_wordCount1 %>%
  arrange(desc(n))%>%
  print(n=15)

Plotting word frequency and rank

ggplot(pride_wordCount1, aes(rank, tf)) + geom_line(linewidth = 1.1, alpha = 0.8, color = "navy")+
  labs(title = "Term Frequency vs. Rank", x = "Rank", y = "Term Frequency")+theme_minimal(base_size = 20)

The relationship is non-linear (or skewed), which means a small number of words are extremely frequent while a great majority of words occur only once or twice.

Handling skewness: Log transformation

ggplot(pride_wordCount1, aes(rank, tf)) + geom_line(linewidth = 1.1, alpha = 0.8, color = "navy")+
  labs(title = "Term Frequency vs. Rank", x = "Rank (log)", y = "Term Frequency (log)")+
  theme_minimal(base_size = 20) + scale_x_log10() + scale_y_log10()

Log transformation highlights the inverse relationship between word frequency and its rank more clearly by scaling the axes to handle the skewed distribution of values.

Zipf’s law

The frequency of a word is inversely proportional to its rank in the frequency table.

\[ frequency \propto \frac{1}{rank} \]

Why is it important?

  • Knowing how words are distributed improves search engines, sentiment analysis, and topic modeling.
    • Sentiment Analysis: Tone and sentiment of a text is detected.
    • Topic modeling: Text topics can be identified.
  • Non-linguistic examples: City populations, species abundance, sports statistics, music/books/media popularity, wealth distribution, etc.

Assignment

  • Download a text of your choice from Project Gutenberg.
  • Tidy the text data.
  • Create a frequency table.
  • Plot the frequency and rank in log-log scale.
  • Write a short report on frequent and infrequent words.

Wrap-up

Text Data Mining

  • Ideal for an introductory undergraduate course
  • Not only for linguistics (grammatical analysis) but also for literature topics (literature analysis, digital humanities)
  • Useful skills for both academic and non-academic tasks

Thank you!

Appendix

Research Areas

Definiteness processing in L1 and L2 English

  • Ahn, H. & Song, M.-J. (2023). L1 Korean speakers’ definiteness processing in L2 English: A visual world paradigm eye-tracking study. Bilingualism: Language and Cognition, 26(3), 556-569.
  • Ahn, H. (2022). L2 Processing of linguistic and non-linguistic information: L2 speakers use definiteness if real-world knowledge is unusable. Studies in Second Language Acquisition, 44(2), 507-535.
  • Ahn, H. (2021). From Interlanguage grammar to target grammar in L2 processing of definiteness as uniqueness. Second Language Research, 37(1), 91-119.

Psych-predicate and thematic role interpretation in Korean and English

  • Ahn, H. & Mariano, G. (accepted). The morphological distribution of English two-place psych predicates in children’s language input. Studies in English Language & Literature, 50(4).
  • Ahn, H. (2024). The argument drop patterns of the psych-predicate mwusep- in spoken, written, and messenger corpora. Studies in English Language & Literature, 50(2), 153-173.
  • Ahn, H. (2020). L2 Korean speakers’ thematic role resolution in a psych-predicate construction. Language Research, 56(2), 141-165.
  • Ahn, H. (2018). Prosody, syntax, and discourse: A three-way interaction. Korean Journal of Linguistics, 43(1), 73-100.
  • Ahn, H. (2017). The prosodic resolution of syntactic/semantic ambiguity: An exemplar-based account. Language Research, 53(3), 501-524.
